# M3.2 editorial corrections

Every change to a previously accepted or previously rejected Game overview, with the text before and after, the reason and the evidence. Changes keep their earlier text in the private dataset.

## Six flagged descriptions (M3.2 §3) (6)

### Snow Brothers: `fbd7caadaf77c2b1`

- **Disposition:** Corrected (exact changes below)
- **Reason:** The NES manual contradicts the arcade-derived details: power-ups are jars of hot sauce that last until a life is lost (not temporary potions); green makes snow travel farther and no yellow jar exists; the flying form comes from a separate special jar; the Flying Pumpkin Head is an ordinary chasing enemy with no stated time-limit trigger or ghost spawning. Snowballs are pushed and rolled (the manual does not mention kicking or setting other snowballs in motion).
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Snow Brothers (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Change:** "Once fully covered, a snowball can be kicked to roll across platforms and rebound off walls, defeating any foes caught in its path and triggering other stationary snowballs into motion." → "(snowballs are pushed and rolled to smash foes; buried foes break free if left too long)"
- **Change:** "Defeated monsters occasionally leave behind colored potion items that grant temporary enhancements." → "(hot sauce jars, powers last until a life is lost)"
- **Change:** "yellow potions extend throwing distance, and green potions temporarily inflate the snowmen into flying forms that knock out adversaries" → "(green: throw farther; separate jars: invincible flying giant, treasure-room warp)"
- **Change:** "Taking excessive time to clear an area summons an invincible pumpkin hazard that produces lethal ghosts." → "(removed; boss round every tenth floor, slot-machine bonus round, SNOW letters)"
- **Check:** claim checker pass (12 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Players control the snowman twins Nick and Tom, hurling snow at hostile monsters to rescue captured princesses. Hurling snow repeatedly encases enemies until they transform into giant snowballs. Once fully covered, a snowball can be kicked to roll across platforms and rebound off walls, defeating any foes caught in its path and triggering other stationary snowballs into motion. ¶ Defeated monsters occasionally leave behind colored potion items that grant temporary enhancements. Red potions increase movement speed, blue potions boost the volume of snow thrown, yellow potions extend throwing distance, and green potions temporarily inflate the snowmen into flying forms that knock out adversaries. Taking excessive time to clear an area summons an invincible pumpkin hazard that produces lethal ghosts.

**After (in production):**

> One or two players control Nick and Tom, the Snow Brothers, who climb floor by floor through King Scorch's Ice Commandos to rescue the kidnapped Frost Princesses. Throwing snow at an enemy packs it into a huge snowball, which can then be pushed and rolled to smash every foe in its path; a buried enemy breaks free if it is left too long. ¶ Some defeated foes drop jars of hot sauce whose powers last until a life is lost: red adds running and jumping speed, blue produces larger globs of snow, and green throws snow farther. Other jars turn a brother into an invincible flying giant or warp him to a treasure room for bonus letters. Every tenth floor is a boss round followed by a slot-machine bonus round, and spelling SNOW earns an extra life.

### Faxanadu: `58f2ba801354a8e5`

**Retained as verified** (owner decision: the Virtual Console product page counts as official Nintendo text). No change.

> Faxanadu combines side-scrolling platforming with action role-playing gameplay. Controlling an adventurer named Nello, the player returns to the troubled Elven town of Eolis at the base of the World Tree. The protagonist sets out to climb the tree, discover why the water supply has stopped, and defeat The Evil One. Progression moves screen by screen through fields, towns, and dungeons across four primary overworld regions: the buttress, the trunk, the branches, and a mountain stronghold. ¶ The character walks, jumps, climbs ladders, strikes foes with bladed weapons, and casts offensive magic projectiles. Defeated adversaries leave behind experience, currency, health-restoring bread, or other items. Experience raises the hero's rank through church Gurus, who also provide password mantras to resume play. Players speak with villagers for information, spend collected gold in shops on usable gear or armor, and monitor status meters for health, magic power, items, and experience.

### Touchdown Fever: `115fcca17cb77d7c`

- **Disposition:** Corrected (exact changes below)
- **Reason:** Owner decision (M3.2 §3.2): the horse-collar clause came from the encyclopedia's arcade gameplay section and is not NES-specific. The remaining claims match the NES manual (12 teams in two divisions, city names, two-player play against the computer, long pass / short pass / QB sneak / backs / punt / field goal formations, two-point conversions by running or passing) and the encyclopedia's NES Version section (field-goal range and automatic success unless blocked).
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Touch Down Fever (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR); encyclopedia "Touchdown Fever" § NES Version
- **Change:** "Teams represent real-world football cities, operating under standard football rules while excluding horse-collar tackles." → "Teams represent real-world football cities."
- **Check:** claim checker pass (8 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Touchdown Fever depicts the sport of American football across matches that support two players. Teams represent real-world football cities, operating under standard football rules while excluding horse-collar tackles. Play encompasses standard running and passing maneuvers alongside special teams kicking. ¶ On offense, human competitors choose from a pool of five basic plays: short passes, long passes, quarterback sneaks, hand-offs to backs, and punts or field goals. A field goal attempt is available only when the line of scrimmage rests within the opponent fifteen-yard line, succeeding automatically unless the opposing defense blocks the kick. Players can also opt to run a two-point conversion.

**After (in production):**

> Touchdown Fever depicts the sport of American football across matches that support two players. Teams represent real-world football cities. Play encompasses standard running and passing maneuvers alongside special teams kicking. ¶ On offense, human competitors choose from a pool of five basic plays: short passes, long passes, quarterback sneaks, hand-offs to backs, and punts or field goals. A field goal attempt is available only when the line of scrimmage rests within the opponent fifteen-yard line, succeeding automatically unless the opposing defense blocks the kick. Players can also opt to run a two-point conversion.

### Starship Hector: `7b7ccdcda705c2c3`

**Retained as verified** (owner decision: 49 words are supported by the evidence; not padded). No change.

> Segments in this scrolling shooter feature both horizontally and vertically moving perspectives. The player pilots a starship through each section while facing hostile targets. ¶ Two distinct firing options are available to the vessel. One weapon configuration targets airborne foes, while the second mode addresses adversaries stationed on the ground.

- **Disposition:** Corrected (optional wording polish)
- **Reason:** Optional wording polish approved in M3.2 §3.5: two stiff phrases simplified; facts unchanged (the source says Dr. Morbis creates the Ape-Man, who is the final boss, fought by Hercules).
- **Evidence:** encyclopedia "The Cheetahmen" § Cheetahmen II
- **Change:** "Dr. Morbis manufactures the Ape-Man" → "Dr. Morbis creates the Ape-Man"
- **Change:** "Hercules subsequently engages the Ape-Man as the concluding adversary of the experience." → "Hercules then faces the Ape-Man as the final boss."
- **Check:** claim checker pass (6 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Guiding the Cheetahmen to defeat Dr. Morbis and his creations forms the primary goal of this title. At the start of the adventure, Dr. Morbis manufactures the Ape-Man to oppose the group. During the Apollo portion, players encounter Dr. Morbis directly as an opponent who continuously dashes toward the right side until beaten. ¶ Progression involves controlling specific characters across designated stages against particular adversaries. Aries navigates two areas where Cygore appears at the end, running back and forth until bested. Hercules subsequently engages the Ape-Man as the concluding adversary of the experience.

**After (in production):**

> Guiding the Cheetahmen to defeat Dr. Morbis and his creations forms the primary goal of this title. At the start, Dr. Morbis creates the Ape-Man to oppose the group. During the Apollo portion, players encounter Dr. Morbis directly as an opponent who continuously dashes toward the right side until beaten. ¶ Progression involves controlling specific characters across designated stages against particular adversaries. Aries navigates two areas where Cygore appears at the end, running back and forth until bested. Hercules then faces the Ape-Man as the final boss.

### Ultimate Basketball: `bd6797920755a1dc`

- **Disposition:** Corrected
- **Reason:** the NES manual contradicts: Fouling out can leave a squad with only three or four competitors remaining on the floor by the end of the match. (manual: Players who receive 5 personal fouls must be replaced; the manual explicitly states that you must have 5 players on the court at all times.)
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Ultimate Basketball (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Contradicted claim:** "Fouling out can leave a squad with only three or four competitors remaining on the floor by the end of the match.". NES manual: Players who receive 5 personal fouls must be replaced; the manual explicitly states that you must have 5 players on the court at all times.
- **Check:** claim checker pass (7 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Modes of play include a tournament mode and a single game mode. Players select a squad from a roster of seven available teams, which feature entirely fictional rosters and fictional statistics rather than real-world professional or college athletes. ¶ On the court, the user directs five squad members, directly maneuvering one teammate at a time. The rules enforce personal violations such as traveling and personal fouls, and any athlete who accumulates five personal fouls is removed from the match. Because five athletes must remain on the court at all times, disqualified players must immediately be replaced with a substitute from the bench.

**After (in production):**

> Modes of play include a tournament mode and a single game mode. Players select a squad from a roster of seven available teams, which feature entirely fictional rosters and fictional statistics rather than real-world professional or college athletes. ¶ On the court, the user directs five squad members, directly maneuvering one teammate at a time. The rules enforce personal violations such as traveling and personal fouls, and any athlete who accumulates five personal fouls is removed from the match. Because five athletes must remain on the court at all times, disqualified players must immediately be replaced with a substitute from the bench.

## Five previously rejected records (M3.2 §4) (5)

### Bram Stoker's Dracula: `a8b6829d8d1e17cc`

- **Disposition:** Rebuilt from the NES manual
- **Reason:** written from nes manual and checked claim by claim against it
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Bram Stoker's Dracula (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Check:** claim checker pass (5 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Set within the narrative of the 1992 film based on the 1897 novel Dracula by Bram Stoker, the game follows the vampire legend. The single player character controlled throughout the action is Jonathan Harker, one of the primary figures from the source material. ¶ Designed as a platform game, the title focuses on Harker as he navigates the setting. His wife, Mina Harker, is absent from the experience entirely.

**After (in production):**

> Taking up the role of Jonathan Harker, the player embarks on a quest to defeat the Prince of Darkness and rescue Mina Murray before she is permanently transformed into a vampire. Across various stages set in woods, castles, and mansions, Harker must traverse hazardous terrain such as nail beds, spike pits, and floating platforms while defeating henchmen, bats, and skeletons under a time limit. ¶ Harker attacks enemies using weapons discovered inside pick-up boxes, including rocks, spinning axes, torches that erupt into flames, and triple rocks. Containers also provide point bonuses, healing items, extra lives, temporary invincibility, and added stage time. Each level culminates in an encounter against Dracula, who attacks in varying incarnations such as a living shadow, a young nobleman, a bat, a wolf beast, or his robed form.

### Faria - A World of Mystery & Danger!: `6f23f245b9edc5af`

- **Disposition:** Corrected (exact changes below)
- **Reason:** Rejected only for the evaluative word "expansive"; replaced with a neutral phrase. All claims passed the claim check.
- **Evidence:** earlier claim check (ver2: fail (evaluative wording only; no unsupported claims))
- **Change:** "across an expansive overworld" → "across an overworld"
- **Check:** claim checker pass (9 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Directing a sword-wielding soldier, the player journeys across the Kingdom of Faria to defeat an evil Wizard and rescue the realm's kidnapped princess. The adventure leads through settlements, across an expansive overworld, and into labyrinthine dungeons spanning northern lands, a cloud domain, and icy caverns. Visiting towns allows the protagonist to speak with non-player characters to gather clues, medicine, and key objects such as the Crystal of Truth. ¶ Progression alternates between wilderness travel and subterranean exploration. Traversing the overworld triggers battles fought on dedicated screens, whereas dungeon encounters happen within the maze navigation display. Combat involves maneuvering the character across the area to strike adversaries with an equipped sword or wield auxiliary armaments, which include bombs and a bow with arrows. Navigating intricate dungeon mazes enables the warrior to conquer guardians, uncover a special sword, and eliminate the Wizard's monster forms.

**After (in production):**

> Directing a sword-wielding soldier, the player journeys across the Kingdom of Faria to defeat an evil Wizard and rescue the realm's kidnapped princess. The adventure leads through settlements, across an overworld, and into labyrinthine dungeons spanning northern lands, a cloud domain, and icy caverns. Visiting towns allows the protagonist to speak with non-player characters to gather clues, medicine, and key objects such as the Crystal of Truth. ¶ Progression alternates between wilderness travel and subterranean exploration. Traversing the overworld triggers battles fought on dedicated screens, whereas dungeon encounters happen within the maze navigation display. Combat involves maneuvering the character across the area to strike adversaries with an equipped sword or wield auxiliary armaments, which include bombs and a bow with arrows. Navigating intricate dungeon mazes enables the warrior to conquer guardians, uncover a special sword, and eliminate the Wizard's monster forms.

### John Elway's Quarterback: `8d21e1e256f82579`

- **Disposition:** Rebuilt from the NES manual
- **Reason:** written from nes manual and checked claim by claim against it
- **Evidence:** NES manual "John Elway's Quarterback (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Check:** claim checker pass (12 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Matches simulate American football across offensive and defensive sequences on the field. Participants choose formations and plays directly from a playbook. On offense, the participant aims to move the ball downfield by passing or kicking, working to release throws before defenders cause a sack. ¶ Playbook menus operate with a timer before each play begins. Choosing the normal play requires the quarterback to throw quickly because the offensive line collapses unusually fast. When the throw is completed, receivers head toward the ball, gain the ability to sprint at high speeds, and attempt to avoid being tackled by opposing defenders.

**After (in production):**

> Matches take place between two squads, the blue team and the red team, starting from a locker room selection screen and running continuously until the contest concludes. The game supports single-player competition against the computer or simultaneous head-to-head play for two participants using separate controllers. ¶ Before each down, participants select offensive or defensive formations from an on-screen menu. On offense, the participant steers the quarterback and can run with the ball or initiate a pass behind the line of scrimmage. Pressing the throw button shifts directional control to an aiming cursor, sending the football toward the chosen point upon release. Kicking maneuvers, such as kickoffs, punts, field goals, and extra points, also rely on cursor placement. While moving, athletes can dive forward to execute lunging tackles, or jump vertically while stationary to deflect or intercept passes. On defense, control primarily governs the middle linebacker, though control can switch to the defender closest to the ball carrier.

### Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles III - The Manhattan Project: `9f671090ffd2bb1d`

- **Disposition:** Corrected (exact changes below)
- **Reason:** The second draft passed the claim check and was held back only for length (161 words). Shortened by merging the control sentences and dropping two minor details (the downward toss attack, borrowing reserve lives); no claim added.
- **Evidence:** earlier claim check (ver2: pass); NES manual "Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles III - The Manhattan Project (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Change:** "alongside a downward toss attack" → "(removed)"
- **Change:** "borrow reserve lives from the second player stock" → "(removed)"
- **Check:** claim checker pass (10 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Players control Leonardo, Raphael, Michelangelo, or Donatello in beat 'em up action, using signature weapons to combat enemy forces. After Shredder kidnaps April O'Neil and lifts Manhattan into the sky as a floating island, the heroes set out to rescue her and halt his scheme. Maneuvers rely on an attack button and a jump button, alongside a downward toss attack. Each turtle also possesses a different special attack executed by pressing both buttons together, which consumes energy unless the character is on their final health bar. ¶ Eight levels progress from Florida beaches to Manhattan, the Technodrome, Shredder's lair, and Krang's Spaceship. Throughout the stages, heroes confront Foot Soldiers, Giant Mousers, Stone Warriors, and bosses, ending against Super Shredder. Up to two players can participate simultaneously, choosing whether attacks harm each other. Depleting the energy gauge removes a life, though players can switch characters after losing a life, borrow reserve lives from the second player stock, and utilize up to three continues.

**After (in production):**

> Players control Leonardo, Raphael, Michelangelo, or Donatello in beat 'em up action, using signature weapons to combat enemy forces. After Shredder kidnaps April O'Neil and lifts Manhattan into the sky as a floating island, the heroes set out to rescue her and halt his scheme. Moves rely on an attack button and a jump button, and each turtle has a different special attack performed by pressing both buttons together, which consumes energy unless the character is on the final health bar. ¶ Eight levels progress from Florida beaches to Manhattan, the Technodrome, Shredder's lair, and Krang's Spaceship. Throughout the stages, heroes confront Foot Soldiers, Giant Mousers, Stone Warriors, and bosses, ending against Super Shredder. Up to two players can participate simultaneously, choosing whether attacks harm each other. Depleting the energy gauge costs a life; players can switch turtles after losing a life and use up to three continues.

### WWF WrestleMania: `a6f98e57ffb45441`

- **Disposition:** Corrected (exact changes below)
- **Reason:** Rejected for the word "unique". The NES manual does not mention the health items in that sentence, so the sentence was removed rather than reworded. The remaining claims match the manual (red anger gauge adds damage, bodyslam rules, turnbuckle moves from the bottom of the ring, fifteen-match round-robin tournaments decided by record and shortest average time).
- **Evidence:** NES manual "WWF Wrestlemania (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Change:** "Unique health icons also appear periodically, though competitors can collect only their designated item." → "(removed)"
- **Check:** claim checker pass (12 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Controlling one of six professional wrestlers, the player competes in matches inside the ring using strikes, running attacks, and bodyslams. Combatants cannot exit the ring, and turnbuckle leaps are limited to the bottom corners. Move sets differ somewhat across the roster, and only Hulk Hogan can bodyslam André the Giant. Taking heavy punishment causes a wrestler to turn red with anger, which temporarily boosts attack strength. Unique health icons also appear periodically, though competitors can collect only their designated item. ¶ Play modes include single exhibitions and tournaments. Exhibitions pit one player against the computer or another participant. In single-player tournaments, the chosen fighter must overcome the other five opponents to earn the championship. Group tournaments allow up to six players across fifteen round-robin bouts, awarding the belt to the best record or breaking ties by the quickest victory.

**After (in production):**

> Controlling one of six professional wrestlers, the player competes in matches inside the ring using strikes, running attacks, and bodyslams. Combatants cannot exit the ring, and turnbuckle leaps are limited to the bottom corners. Move sets differ somewhat across the roster, and only Hulk Hogan can bodyslam André the Giant. Taking heavy punishment causes a wrestler to turn red with anger, which temporarily boosts attack strength. ¶ Play modes include single exhibitions and tournaments. Exhibitions pit one player against the computer or another participant. In single-player tournaments, the chosen fighter must overcome the other five opponents to earn the championship. Group tournaments allow up to six players across fifteen round-robin bouts, awarding the belt to the best record or breaking ties by the quickest victory.

## Audit corrections of accepted overviews: corrected by hand from the NES manual (12)

### Arkanoid: `a3763e702f8ae081`

- **Disposition:** Corrected
- **Reason:** Conflict: the audit read the NES manual as 33 rounds and the encyclopedia states 36 for the NES; the manual's number is unreadable in the OCR. The count was removed rather than guessed.
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Arkanoid (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Contradicted claim:** "The game takes place across 36 stages.". NES manual: There are 33 rounds in this game (with a level select trick going up to the 16th round).
- **Check:** claim checker pass (7 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Across 36 stages, gameplay centers on clearing formations of bricks by bouncing a ball off a paddle-shaped craft called the Vaus. The craft moves horizontally across the bottom of the screen to prevent the ball from dropping off the playfield. While many bricks shatter on contact, others require several strikes to break, and some cannot be destroyed. Eliminating all target blocks allows the player to advance to the next arrangement, culminating in a confrontation against the boss entity DOH on the final stage. ¶ Shattering certain blocks releases power-up capsules that alter mechanics when collected. These items can lengthen the Vaus, equip it with cannons, slow the ball, yield extra lives, or spawn multiple balls. Floating obstacles also drift into the play area and can be struck for additional points.

**After (in production):**

> Across a series of rounds, gameplay centers on clearing formations of bricks by bouncing a ball off a paddle-shaped craft called the Vaus. The craft moves horizontally across the bottom of the screen to prevent the ball from dropping off the playfield. While many bricks shatter on contact, others require several strikes to break, and some cannot be destroyed. Eliminating all target blocks allows the player to advance to the next arrangement, culminating in a confrontation against the boss entity DOH on the final stage. ¶ Shattering certain blocks releases power-up capsules that alter mechanics when collected. These items can lengthen the Vaus, equip it with cannons, slow the ball, yield extra lives, or spawn multiple balls. Floating obstacles also drift into the play area and can be struck for additional points.

### Bump 'n' Jump: `ba8c9990f378b941`

- **Disposition:** Corrected
- **Reason:** The earlier text gave 100 mph (the arcade figure); the NES manual gives 160 km/h. The speed was generalized rather than restating an OCR-read number.
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Bump'n'Jump (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Contradicted claim:** "Once the vehicle reaches a speed of at least 100 mph, the driver can jump.". NES manual: The manual states the car must reach a speed of 100 km/hour (not mph) to jump.
- **Check:** claim checker pass (9 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Bump 'n' Jump is a vehicular combat game for one player. The objective is to steer a car to the end of a course while navigating past enemy traffic and over obstacles. Once the vehicle reaches a speed of at least 100 mph, the driver can jump to cross large hazards such as bodies of water, which are preceded by a flashing exclamation point warning. ¶ Enemy traffic consists of cars and trucks. Cars can be bumped into the sides of the track or crushed from above. Trucks cannot be bumped and occasionally drop debris that must be avoided, though landing on them also destroys them. Colliding with track borders, plunging into water, or hitting obstacles costs a car.

**After (in production):**

> Bump 'n' Jump is a vehicular combat game for one player. The objective is to steer a car to the end of a course while navigating past enemy traffic and over obstacles. Once the vehicle is moving fast enough, the driver can jump to cross large hazards such as bodies of water, which are preceded by a flashing exclamation point warning. ¶ Enemy traffic consists of cars and trucks. Cars can be bumped into the sides of the track or crushed from above. Trucks cannot be bumped and occasionally drop debris that must be avoided, though landing on them also destroys them. Colliding with track borders, plunging into water, or hitting obstacles costs a car.

### Cybernoid - The Fighting Machine: `ad1e14d08657d99c`

- **Disposition:** Corrected
- **Reason:** The NES manual: each room has its own time bomb that explodes if the ship does not leave in time (not an overall time limit for returning the haul). Automated correction accepted with "unique" reworded.
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Cybernoid - The Fighting Machine (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Contradicted claim:** "The objective requires returning the haul before a time limit expires.". NES manual: Each room has its own time bomb that explodes if the player does not exit within the designated time limit, rather than having to return the haul before an overall time limit expires.
- **Check:** claim checker pass (12 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Piloting the Cybernoid ship, the player navigates flip-screen environments to battle pirates and planetary defense systems across a single-player mission. The objective requires retrieving stolen minerals, jewels, ammunition, and weaponry taken from Federation storage depots, then returning the haul before a time limit expires. Each area presents diverse hazards, including enemy vessels, homing missiles, stationary gun turrets, and invulnerable obstacles moving along fixed routes. ¶ Destroying hostile craft frequently produces jewels or power-ups for the ship to gather. Available enhancements include protective shields, homing missiles, a rear-firing weapon, and an orbiting spiked ball that aids in defense.

**After (in production):**

> Piloting the Cybernoid ship, the player maneuvers across flip-screen environments to battle pirates and planetary defenses during a single-player mission. The objective involves reclaiming stolen cargo from Federation storage asteroids while surviving hostile chambers. Each room contains a time bomb with its own countdown that will detonate if the craft fails to exit before time expires, preceded by pulsating light warnings. Threats throughout the screens include enemy craft, missiles, ground emplacements, and invulnerable obstacles following preset paths. ¶ Eliminating hostile forces frequently yields jewels, minerals, or supply crates for the craft to gather. Alongside a default forward blaster, an arsenal of selected auxiliary weapons includes bombs, screen-clearing genocide missiles, temporary defensive shields, bouncing ordnance, and ground-tracking seekers. Special attachments can also be retrieved, including a rear gun and orbiting CyberMace spheres.

### Ghosts'n Goblins: `eea66f7bcc90d114`

- **Disposition:** Corrected
- **Reason:** The NES manual describes seven guarded gates (the earlier text said six stages). Automated correction accepted with "save his beloved" reworded (the lint reads "beloved" as evaluative).
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Ghosts'n Goblins (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Contradicted claim:** "Six monster-infested stages structure the journey to confront the Devil.". NES manual: There are seven guarded gates in total to rescue the Princess.

**Before:**

> Six monster-infested stages structure the journey to confront the Devil. The player controls the knight Sir Arthur on a quest to rescue Princess Prin-Prin from supernatural captors. Arthur must defeat various adversaries, including zombies, giants, demons, cyclopes, and dragons, while racing against a set time limit for each level. ¶ Taking two hits costs Arthur a life, sending the player back to a checkpoint or the beginning of the stage. Players can collect bonus points, extra suits of armor, and alternative weapons like the javelin, dagger, and axe. Certain weapons cannot harm specific bosses. Conquering the Devil requires navigating through the entire set of stages a second time at a higher difficulty to view the final ending.

**After (in production):**

> Seven guarded gates structure the journey to rescue the princess and confront the Devil. The player controls the knight Arthur on his quest against supernatural captors. Arthur must defeat various adversaries, including zombies, giants, demons, cyclopes, and dragons, while racing against a set time limit for each level. ¶ Taking two hits costs Arthur a life, sending the player back to a checkpoint or the beginning of the stage. Players can collect bonus points, extra suits of armor, and alternative weapons like the javelin, dagger, and axe. Certain weapons cannot harm specific bosses. Conquering the Devil requires navigating through the stages a second time at a higher difficulty to view the final ending.

### Jurassic Park: `49fe0c49cd0a2841`

- **Disposition:** Corrected
- **Reason:** The NES manual shows the Tyrannosaurus rex can be fired upon and its power is tracked by a meter (the earlier text said it cannot be defeated and must be avoided). The automated correction was accepted except for the word "unique", replaced by neutral wording.
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Jurassic Park (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Contradicted claim:** "The Tyrannosaurus rex cannot be defeated, must be avoided, and appears as an end boss across select levels.". NES manual: The T-Rex can be fired upon and damaged (its power is tracked by a meter) in Level 2.
- **Check:** claim checker pass (11 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Guiding Dr. Alan Grant from an overhead perspective, the player shoots hostile creatures and navigates a dinosaur park after a containment breakdown on Isla Nublar. The overarching goal involves rescuing John Hammond's grandchildren, restoring the park's computer systems, clearing out Velociraptor nests with time bombs, and reaching an extraction point. Across six levels composed of multiple stages, the player gathers dinosaur eggs and access cards needed to progress, utilizing guns found across each area to eliminate common threats such as Compsognathus and Dilophosaurus. ¶ Certain foes cannot be defeated and must be avoided, including a Tyrannosaurus rex that appears as an end boss across select levels. Scattered mystery boxes impart various outcomes, such as providing extra lives, invincibility, or health replenishment, though some containers deplete energy or remove a life. Grant operates with three lives alongside four continues. A two-player option allows two participants to play by alternating turns.

**After (in production):**

> Guiding Dr. Alan Grant from an overhead perspective, the player shoots hostile creatures and navigates a dinosaur park after a containment breakdown on Isla Nublar. The overarching goal involves rescuing John Hammond's grandchildren, restoring the park's computer systems, clearing out Velociraptor nests with time bombs, and reaching an extraction point. Across six levels composed of multiple stages, the player gathers dinosaur eggs and access cards needed to progress, utilizing guns found across each area to eliminate prehistoric threats such as Compsognathus and Dilophosaurus. ¶ Some encounters add other tasks, such as protecting the children from charging herds or firing upon a Tyrannosaurus rex whose stamina is tracked with a power meter. Scattered mystery boxes impart various outcomes, such as providing extra lives, invincibility, or health replenishment, though some containers deplete energy or remove a life. Grant operates with three lives alongside four continues. A two-player option allows two participants to cooperate by alternating turns whenever a life is lost.

### Last Action Hero: `f4b7d8d31c5bbfee`

- **Disposition:** Corrected
- **Reason:** The earlier text said the game has no vehicle sequences and no final confrontation; the NES manual lists seven scenes, including Scene 4 "The Car Chase" (stop Benedict's motorcade) and a finale against Death. The encyclopedia says the NES version has no driving levels, so the scene is described without claiming the player drives.
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Last Action Hero (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Contradicted claim:** "Progression occurs entirely without vehicle pursuit sequences.". NES manual: Scene 4 is explicitly titled 'The Car Chase' where the player must take out Benedict's monster motorcade.
- **Check:** claim checker pass (11 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Advancing through a series of action levels based on the motion picture serves as the main goal of the game. Progression occurs entirely without vehicle pursuit sequences. ¶ Players navigate through multiple areas, including a stage that portrays Danny Madigan's interpretation of the Shakespeare play Hamlet. The game concludes without a climactic confrontation against the villain Mr. Benedict.

**After (in production):**

> Advancing through a series of action levels based on the motion picture serves as the main goal of the game. Players control movie hero Jack Slater as he pursues the villain Benedict across both fictional and real-world environments. The adventure spans seven scenes, including one titled The Car Chase, in which Slater must stop Benedict's motorcade, and a stage depicting Danny Madigan's interpretation of the Shakespeare play Hamlet. ¶ Slater walks, crouches, jumps, and kicks hostile forces across side-scrolling areas such as school rooftops, chapels, and theater interiors. The protagonist starts with five lives and can collect heart pick-ups to replenish lost health, culminating in an encounter where players face Death rather than Benedict.

### Lode Runner: `6c17c7bf2f7466eb`

- **Disposition:** Corrected
- **Reason:** The NES manual describes 50 treasury rooms (the earlier text said 150). "Jumping is impossible" and "five lives" are not supported by the NES manual (five lives came from the computer original's article) and were removed or generalized.
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Lode Runner (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Contradicted claim:** "The game spans 150 stages.". NES manual: The game has 50 different treasury rooms (50 levels).
- **Check:** claim checker pass (11 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Spanning 150 stages, this action-puzzle platformer presents multi-story brick environments filled with ladders, suspended hand-to-hand bars, and trapdoors. Players control a Galactic Commando tasked with collecting every piece of gold in each level and reaching the top of the screen to advance. While running through the stage, the commando must avoid enemy robots or guards that pursue the character and can also pick up gold bars. The character can fall from any height unharmed, though jumping is impossible. ¶ Defense relies on digging holes into brick surfaces to the character's sides, temporarily trapping pursuing guards and causing them to drop carried gold. Holes eventually refill, consuming trapped enemies and causing them to respawn elsewhere, but digging through bedrock is prohibited. Players begin with five lives, losing one if caught by an enemy or trapped without an escape. Alongside the primary mode, an included edit mode allows players to create customized stages.

**After (in production):**

> Spanning 50 stages, this action-puzzle platformer presents multi-story brick environments filled with ladders, suspended hand-to-hand bars, and trapdoors. Players control a Galactic Commando tasked with collecting every piece of gold in each level and reaching the top of the screen to advance. While running through the stage, the commando must avoid enemy robots or guards that pursue the character and can also pick up gold bars. The character can drop from any height unharmed. ¶ Defense relies on digging holes into brick surfaces to the character's sides, temporarily trapping pursuing guards and causing them to drop carried gold. Holes eventually refill, consuming trapped enemies and causing them to respawn elsewhere, but digging through bedrock is prohibited. A life is lost when an enemy catches the commando or no escape remains. Alongside the primary mode, an included edit mode allows players to create customized stages.

### Star Wars - The Empire Strikes Back: `01b1bc93f72286a1`

- **Disposition:** Corrected
- **Reason:** The encyclopedia says the game ends by rescuing Han Solo and defeating Darth Vader; the audit read the NES manual as saying Han cannot be saved and Vader need only be survived, but the manual's OCR is unreadable at that point. Neither version could be confirmed, so the ending is stated only as far as all sources agree (Cloud City, a confrontation with Darth Vader).
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Star Wars - The Empire Strikes Back (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Contradicted claim:** "To complete the game, the player must rescue Han Solo.". NES manual: The manual states Han has already been frozen in carbonite and Boba Fett has taken him to Jabba the Hutt; you must follow Boba Fett to his ship, but rescuing Han is not accomplished as he cannot be saved in time.
- **Contradicted claim:** "To complete the game, the player must defeat Darth Vader in direct combat.". NES manual: The manual states Luke must face Darth Vader and that Luke does not have to defeat Vader to escape, only survive the duel to escape.
- **Check:** claim checker pass (12 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Players control Luke Skywalker as he fights enemies using a blaster pistol or a lightsaber. Set within the events of the film, the adventure tasks the player with multiple objectives throughout various environments. These goals include destroying an Imperial Probe Droid, finding Master Yoda on Dagobah for training, and escaping an ice cavern infested with Wampas. Luke also participates in the Battle of Hoth, during which he can pilot a snowspeeder. As Luke grows stronger in the Force, he gains multiple Force powers that provide assistance. ¶ Additional mission goals involve attempting to save allies located in Cloud City from Darth Vader. To complete the game, the player must accomplish two final tasks: rescuing Han Solo and defeating Darth Vader in direct combat.

**After (in production):**

> Players control Luke Skywalker as he fights enemies using a blaster pistol or a lightsaber. Set within the events of the film, the adventure tasks the player with multiple objectives throughout various environments. These goals include destroying an Imperial Probe Droid, finding Master Yoda on Dagobah for training, and escaping an ice cavern infested with Wampas. Luke also participates in the Battle of Hoth, during which he can pilot a snowspeeder. As Luke grows stronger in the Force, he gains multiple Force powers that provide assistance. ¶ Later missions take Luke to Cloud City, where his allies are in danger and the journey leads to a confrontation with Darth Vader.

### Super Sprint: `d1defc7a6c6f96f8`

- **Disposition:** Corrected
- **Reason:** The NES manual: races are five laps and two wrenches customize the car (the earlier text said three laps and sets of three). The helicopter replacement is an arcade feature not in the NES manual and was removed.
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Super Sprint (USA) (Unl)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Contradicted claim:** "Each race requires drivers to complete three laps.". NES manual: Races are five laps long.
- **Contradicted claim:** "Drivers trade sets of three wrenches to increase top speed, boost acceleration, or improve tire traction.". NES manual: Two wrenches are needed to customize the car (two appear in each race).
- **Check:** claim checker pass (10 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Circuits viewed from an overhead perspective frame the competitive action, with each race requiring drivers to complete three laps. Competitors guide Formula One-style vehicles across tracks that fit entirely on the screen without scrolling. The winner of a race earns advancement to the next course, while computer-controlled opponents contest the remaining positions. Up to two players can compete. ¶ Hazards populate the courses as participants progress, including miniature tornadoes and puddles of oil that cause temporary loss of steering control. High-speed collisions with walls or drops from elevated bridges destroy a vehicle, prompting a helicopter to deliver a replacement. Drivers can also gather wrenches scattered along the road surface, trading sets of three to increase top speed, boost acceleration, or improve tire traction.

**After (in production):**

> Circuits viewed from an overhead perspective frame the competitive action, with each race requiring drivers to complete five laps. Competitors guide Formula One-style vehicles across tracks that fit entirely on the screen without scrolling. The winner of a race earns advancement to the next course, while computer-controlled opponents contest the remaining positions. Up to two players can compete simultaneously. ¶ Hazards populate the courses as participants progress, including miniature tornadoes and puddles of oil that cause temporary loss of steering control. Drivers can also gather wrenches scattered along the road surface, trading a pair collected during a winning heat to increase top speed, boost acceleration, or improve tire traction.

### Terra Cresta: `667bcf3ca1ebcf6f`

- **Disposition:** Corrected
- **Reason:** The NES manual names the threat as the devil Mandora and his monsters (not the Mandler army). Automated correction accepted with "great devil" shortened to "devil" (the lint read "great" as evaluative).
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Terra Cresta (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Contradicted claim:** "Humanity faces destruction at the hands of the hostile Mandler army.". NES manual: The threat is a great devil named Mandora and monsters under his orders.
- **Check:** claim checker pass (8 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Humanity faces destruction at the hands of the hostile Mandler army. Taking control of the Wing Galibur fighter craft, the player flies into vertical combat to eliminate opposing aerial forces and ground targets. ¶ Destroying ground capsules yields additional ship modules that connect directly to the craft. Up to four modular attachments grant expanded shot coverage and weaponry, such as double shots or a wave gun, while also shielding the vessel from incoming strikes. Pressing the transform command rearranges collected attachments into an invulnerable triangular formation around the vulnerable core ship. When fully assembled with every component, the craft temporarily morphs into an invincible flaming phoenix.

**After (in production):**

> A devil named Mandora and his monster forces have driven humanity into the ocean depths. Taking control of the Wing Galibur fighter craft, the player flies into vertical combat to eliminate opposing aerial forces and ground targets in an effort to reclaim the Earth. ¶ Destroying ground capsules yields additional ship modules that connect directly to the craft. Up to four modular attachments grant expanded shot coverage and weaponry, such as double shots or a wave gun, while also shielding the vessel from incoming strikes. Pressing the transform command rearranges collected attachments into an invulnerable triangular formation around the vulnerable core ship. When fully assembled with every component, the craft temporarily morphs into an invincible flaming phoenix.

> Toki provides run and gun platforming gameplay. Controlling a human tribesman transformed into an ape by an evil wizard, the player journeys across diverse environments to rescue an abducted princess and break the curse. Toki moves slowly and remains vulnerable to enemy attacks, but he can defend himself by firing projectiles from his mouth to defeat hostile creatures and overcome obstacles. ¶ Progress involves navigating timed stages that conclude with boss encounters. Along the way, players can gather items to assist their advance, including spit power-ups, protective headgear, jumping boosts, clocks to replenish remaining time, keys for bonus zones, extra lives, and point items. Two players are supported.

**After (in production):**

> Toki provides run and gun platforming gameplay. Controlling a human tribesman transformed into an ape by an evil wizard, the player journeys across diverse environments to rescue an abducted princess and break the curse. Toki moves slowly and remains vulnerable to enemy attacks, but he can defend himself by firing projectiles from his mouth to defeat hostile creatures and overcome obstacles. ¶ Progress involves navigating timed stages that conclude with boss encounters. Along the way, players can gather items to assist their advance, including weapon power-ups, protective headgear, jumping boosts, clocks to replenish remaining time, keys to open locked rooms, extra lives, and point items.

### Vice - Project Doom: `433f6bcbd59a9d59`

- **Disposition:** Corrected
- **Reason:** Audit: the manual calls the grenades round, not stick grenades (wording generalized to grenades). The audit's second finding (two gears) was wrong: the NES manual says SHIFT GEARS (1 SLOW, 3 FAST), so the three gears stay.
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Vice - Project Doom (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Contradicted claim:** "Hart uses stick grenades.". NES manual: The manual calls them round grenades.
- **Contradicted claim:** "Overhead driving segments require steering an automobile across roads, shifting between three gears, and firing a mounted gun at enemy vehicles.". NES manual: The car has two gear settings: 1 is slow and 2 is fast.
- **Check:** claim checker pass (11 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Taking control of secret agent Detective Hart, the player battles adversaries across eleven stages to investigate an alien conspiracy involving a corporation and an illicit substance. Most levels feature side-scrolling platforming where Hart jumps, crouches, crawls while running, climbs ladders, and uses weapons. Hart switches among an unlimited short-range laser whip, a revolver, and stick grenades. Slain enemies drop ammunition refills, health-restoring items like meat, and coins that yield an extra life for every one hundred collected. ¶ Two other gameplay formats appear throughout the mission. Overhead driving segments require steering an automobile across roads, shifting between three gears, and firing a mounted gun at enemy vehicles. In first-person shooting sections, the player aims a crosshair across moving backgrounds to shoot and throw grenades. Story sequences introduce each stage, and unlimited continues are available once Hart expends his limited stock of lives.

**After (in production):**

> Taking control of secret agent Detective Hart, the player battles adversaries across eleven stages to investigate an alien conspiracy involving a corporation and an illicit substance. Most levels feature side-scrolling platforming where Hart jumps, crouches, crawls while running, climbs ladders, and uses weapons. Hart switches among an unlimited short-range laser whip, a revolver, and grenades. Slain enemies drop ammunition refills, health-restoring items like meat, and coins that yield an extra life for every one hundred collected. ¶ Two other gameplay formats appear throughout the mission. Overhead driving segments require steering an automobile across roads, shifting between three gears, and firing a mounted gun at enemy vehicles. In first-person shooting sections, the player aims a crosshair across moving backgrounds to shoot and throw grenades. Story sequences introduce each stage, and unlimited continues are available once Hart expends his limited stock of lives.

> Destroying the opposing fleet across the Pacific theater of World War II forms the core objective of the mission. Piloting a P-38 Lightning aircraft, the player targets hostile air and sea forces following an assault on an American carrier. Survival depends on managing a depleting fuel meter that serves as health, which empties constantly over time unless replenished. ¶ Controlling the fighter in vertical flight, the pilot fires standard guns, executes aerial loop maneuvers, and can trigger special lightning attacks by spending fuel. Eliminating entire squadrons of red aircraft yields item drops that can be shot to cycle between health boosts and alternate weapons, including shotguns, three-way shots, burst fire, and heavy shells.

### Addams Family, The - Pugsley's Scavenger Hunt: `b3e09c94e33e0293`

- **Disposition:** Corrected
- **Reason:** the NES manual contradicts: The story centers on Pugsley accepting a scavenger hunt challenge issued by his sister, Wednesday. (manual: Pugsley's family has been kidnapped and hidden as prisoners in their mansion, and Pugsley must rescue them.); Pugsley must explore the bedrooms belonging to each member of the Addams family and locate a specific item from every room. (manual: Pugsley must explore the mansion to defeat boss monsters and rescue his kidnapped family members, who then gather in the music room.)
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Addams Family, The - Pugsley's Scavenger Hunt (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Contradicted claim:** "The story centers on Pugsley accepting a scavenger hunt challenge issued by his sister, Wednesday.". NES manual: Pugsley's family has been kidnapped and hidden as prisoners in their mansion, and Pugsley must rescue them.
- **Contradicted claim:** "Pugsley must explore the bedrooms belonging to each member of the Addams family and locate a specific item from every room.". NES manual: Pugsley must explore the mansion to defeat boss monsters and rescue his kidnapped family members, who then gather in the music room.
- **Check:** claim checker pass (8 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> The Addams Family: Pugsley's Scavenger Hunt provides side-scrolling platform gameplay. Based on an animated series, the story follows Pugsley after kidnappers capture his family and conceal them inside their own residence. To save them, Pugsley must explore the grounds and mansion interior to defeat large boss monsters and liberate Gomez, Granny, Wednesday, and Uncle Fester. Each rescued relative gathers in the music room and supplies a portion of a tune for Lurch to play on the organ, unlocking a passage to rescue Morticia. ¶ Players navigate through the estate by entering various doors in the Hall of Stairs in any order. Pugsley runs, jumps, crouches, and swims while defeating enemies by leaping onto them. Along the way, players can hit item boxes to receive hints from Thing, uncover hidden paths, operate switch blocks, and collect power-ups including flying beanies and running shoes.

**After (in production):**

> The Addams Family: Pugsley's Scavenger Hunt provides side-scrolling platform gameplay. Based on an animated series, the story follows Pugsley after kidnappers capture his family and conceal them inside their own residence. To save them, Pugsley must explore the grounds and mansion interior to defeat large boss monsters and liberate Gomez, Granny, Wednesday, and Uncle Fester. Each rescued relative gathers in the music room and supplies a portion of a tune for Lurch to play on the organ, unlocking a passage to rescue Morticia. ¶ Players navigate through the estate by entering various doors in the Hall of Stairs in any order. Pugsley runs, jumps, crouches, and swims while defeating enemies by leaping onto them. Along the way, players can hit item boxes to receive hints from Thing, uncover hidden paths, operate switch blocks, and collect power-ups including flying beanies and running shoes.

### Advanced Dungeons & Dragons - Heroes of the Lance: `df2609c80e818bb9`

(manual: The lead character is the only character that moves and fights on screen, but when a character is slain, their body is left on the screen.); Other party members primarily serve as reserve lives. (manual: All four front-rank characters can take damage from monster attacks, cast certain spells, or be swapped into the lead using Hero Select.)
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Advanced Dungeons & Dragons - Heroes of the Lance (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Contradicted claim:** "Only one party member appears on the screen at any given moment.". NES manual: The lead character is the only character that moves and fights on screen, but when a character is slain, their body is left on the screen.
- **Contradicted claim:** "Other party members primarily serve as reserve lives.". NES manual: All four front-rank characters can take damage from monster attacks, cast certain spells, or be swapped into the lead using Hero Select.
- **Check:** claim checker pass (7 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Retrieving the sacred Disks of Mishakal from the ruined city of Xak Tsaroth forms the ultimate goal, requiring a confrontation with the ancient dragon Khisanth. A single player leads an adventuring party through this fantasy setting within a side-scrolling action format. ¶ The lead character is the only party member that moves and attacks on screen, though fallen heroes leave their bodies behind. Rather than remaining passive reserves, all four front-rank companions can sustain damage from hostile strikes, and heroes can be swapped into the lead position via menu options. Individual adventurers utilize varied abilities based on role-playing statistics, ranging from close combat weaponry and ranged bows to defensive healing spells and offensive wizard magic.

> Thirty-two stages comprise the journey, split into eight distinct areas made up of four rounds each. Higgins possesses a health meter that constantly depletes over time or when tripping on rocks. - **Contradicted claim:** "The player guides a green creature named Pogo.". NES manual: The protagonist is an Anobian hybrid named Julius.
- **Check:** claim checker pass (9 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Set across multiple cylindrical sea towers, this single-player platformer tasks players with guiding a green creature named Pogo to the summit of each structure. The player starts at the bottom of a tower and works upward along exterior ledges, climbing steps and taking elevators. As Pogo walks left or right, the tower rotates in the opposite direction. ¶ Various geometric adversaries obstruct the ascent. Pogo can fire at certain enemies, while others resist attacks. Touching an obstacle knocks the character down to a lower platform, and falling past the lowest level into the sea results in drowning. Reaching a door at the peak triggers a bomb that demolishes the tower.

**After (in production):**

> Set across eight cylindrical sea towers, the game tasks the player with guiding a character named Julius to the summit of each structure. Julius begins at the bottom of a tower and works upward along exterior ledges, climbing stairs and riding elevators. As the protagonist walks left or right, the cylindrical tower rotates, creating a sense of depth. ¶ Various geometric adversaries obstruct the ascent. Julius can shoot and stun or destroy certain hazards, while others resist all attacks. Contact with an enemy knocks the character down to a lower ledge, and tumbling into the water below causes the loss of a life. Reaching a door at the peak triggers a bomb to demolish the tower, after which Julius visits a diamond-studded bonus level to collect gems.

### Challenge of the Dragon: `c20a8cc1b2dacb8a`

- **Disposition:** Corrected

- **Check:** claim checker pass (10 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Challenge of the Dragon is an action game centered on martial arts combat. Players take control of Sir Burkelot, a Dragon Style Master on a mission to rescue his companion, Lady Winita, who has been abducted by the necromancer Femiwind. Sir Burkelot traverses multiple distinct environments, starting in a village and advancing through areas such as multi-leveled zones, castle walls, forests, and inner strongholds. Along the journey, he contends with mind-controlled adversaries including ninjas, samurai, giants, wizards, and skeletons. ¶ Combat relies on a context-sensitive system where pressing the attack button triggers different martial arts techniques depending on proximity to a foe. Techniques include sword strikes, close-range knee strikes, side kicks, a shoulder butt performed during a super run, and a jumping dragon kick. The hero can also cast collected magic potions. Colored potion items restore health, defeat foes, or provide an extra life. Sir Burkelot's remaining vitality and lives are shown on an overhead status bar.

**After (in production):**

### Hudson Hawk: `3991a761116131dc`

- **Disposition:** Corrected
- **Reason:** the NES manual contradicts: Hawk is dispatched to steal three Da Vinci artifacts. (manual: The Mayflowers order Hawk to steal two priceless works by Leonardo da Vinci (one in Rutherford's auction house, the other in the Vatican), and later steal the key component of the gold machine.); The protagonist can neutralize adversaries by launching tennis balls at them. (manual: Hawk throws softballs, not tennis balls.)
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Hudson Hawk (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Change:** "cat burglar Eddie Hawkins" → "cat burglar Hawkins" (The NES manual's OCR names Hawkins only; the first name was not in the source.)
- **Contradicted claim:** "Hawk is dispatched to steal three Da Vinci artifacts.". NES manual: The Mayflowers order Hawk to steal two priceless works by Leonardo da Vinci (one in Rutherford's auction house, the other in the Vatican), and later steal the key component of the gold machine.
- **Contradicted claim:** "The protagonist can neutralize adversaries by launching tennis balls at them.". NES manual: Hawk throws softballs, not tennis balls.
- **Check:** claim checker pass (11 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Levels in this single-player platformer structure the adventure of Hudson Hawk, a cat burglar dispatched to steal three Da Vinci artifacts. ¶ Controlling Hawk, the player must explore each stage while taking care to avoid triggering alarms. Guard dogs and security personnel actively patrol the areas to disrupt the heist. To deal with these threats, the protagonist can neutralize adversaries by punching them or by launching tennis balls at them.

**After (in production):**

> Three levels structure this single-player platformer, sending cat burglar Hawkins to steal two priceless Leonardo da Vinci works and the key component of an alchemy machine. ¶ Controlling Hawk, the player runs, jumps, and crawls through tight passages while taking care to avoid triggering alarms, electric eyes, or floor panels. Security personnel and other obstacles hinder progress across each area. To deal with hostile threats, the protagonist relies on his fists for close-range punches and can throw softballs to stun or knock foes off the screen. Softballs also trigger switches that momentarily disable electrical hazards, aiding Hawk as he searches for designated targets and navigates elevators, ropes, and platforms.

### ImageFight: `7032a94d140339f9`

- **Disposition:** Corrected
- **Reason:** the NES manual contradicts: Earth faces conquest by extraterrestrial invaders from the Boondoggle Galaxy. (manual: The manual states the invaders are from the Booglie Galaxy (OCR: Fooglt / Booglie).)
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Image Fight (USA)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Change:** " from the Booglie Galaxy" → "(removed)" ("Booglie Galaxy" was the model's reading of garbled manual OCR ("Fooglt"); the galaxy name was removed.)
- **Contradicted claim:** "Earth faces conquest by extraterrestrial invaders from the Boondoggle Galaxy.". NES manual: The manual states the invaders are from the Booglie Galaxy (OCR: Fooglt / Booglie).
- **Check:** claim checker pass (10 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> ImageFight represents a vertically scrolling shooter in which Earth faces conquest by extraterrestrial invaders from the Boondoggle Galaxy. Set in the year 20XX after an assault shatters the moon, the premise tasks pilots with repelling enemy forces using the advanced OF-1 Fightership. Up to two players can participate in this conflict. ¶ The player navigates a red combat craft through vertically advancing environments filled with hostile targets. Action commences across combat simulation levels that evaluate performance based on destroyed targets, judging whether the final score meets passing requirements. Maintaining an average destruction rate of at least ninety percent grants direct access to real combat missions, while falling below that threshold triggers a supplementary stage. The ensuing combat phases progress sequentially upon clearing each area.

**After (in production):**

> ImageFight represents a vertically scrolling shooter in which Earth faces conquest by extraterrestrial invaders. Set in the year 20XX after an assault shatters the moon, the premise tasks pilots with repelling enemy forces using the advanced OF-1 Fightership. Up to two players can participate in this conflict. ¶ The player navigates a red combat craft through vertically advancing environments filled with hostile targets. Action commences across combat simulation levels that evaluate performance based on destroyed targets, judging whether the final score meets passing requirements. Maintaining an average destruction rate of at least ninety percent grants direct access to real combat missions, while falling below that threshold triggers supplementary training. The ensuing combat phases progress sequentially upon clearing each area.

### King of Kings, The - The Early Years: `3249d267ca170980`

- **Disposition:** Corrected

- **Change:** "the journey of the wise men to Bethlehem" → "the journey of the wise men to visit the infant Jesus" (Bethlehem is not stated in the sources; the encyclopedia article on the NES game describes the wise men journeying to visit the infant Jesus. The other places and names (Egypt, Joseph and Mary, Jerusalem) are confirmed by that article; the manual OCR names Jerusalem.)
- **Check:** claim checker pass (8 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> The King of Kings: The Early Years offers three separate adventures based on biblical events. Players select between the journey of the wise men to Bethlehem, the flight of Joseph and Mary to Egypt, and the search for young Jesus in Jerusalem. Across each game, running into creatures or obstacles causes the player to lose scrolls, which serve as health. Collecting a scroll and correctly answering a multiple-choice or true-or-false Bible trivia question replenishes the scroll counter, whereas losing every scroll ends the game. ¶ In the first adventure, the player steers wise men riding camels, spitting water balls or gathered fruit projectiles at hazards while collecting frankincense, myrrh, and gold. The second scenario involves steering a donkey across terrains with falling rocks, logs, and hostile wildlife. The third scenario tasks Joseph and Mary with traversing stages before a timer expires.

**After (in production):**

Players select between the journey of the wise men to visit the infant Jesus, the flight of Joseph and Mary to Egypt, and the search for young Jesus in Jerusalem. Collecting a scroll and correctly answering a multiple-choice or true-or-false Bible trivia question replenishes the scroll counter, whereas losing every scroll ends the game. ¶ In the first adventure, the player steers wise men riding camels, spitting water balls or gathered fruit projectiles at hazards while collecting frankincense, myrrh, and gold. The second scenario involves steering a donkey across terrains with falling rocks, logs, and hostile wildlife. The third scenario tasks Joseph and Mary with traversing stages before a timer expires.

In F-15 City War, the player pilots a fighter jet across five stages to destroy enemy tanks, helicopters, and robots using guns and missiles. Puzzle requires unscrambling randomized tiles within a time limit, with an option to challenge the computer to minigames to swap pieces. Tiles of Fate entails connecting matching pairs under movement path restrictions before time runs out, while Krazy Kreatures tasks the player with aligning rows of encroaching monsters.

### Mermaids of Atlantis - The Riddle of the Magic Bubble: `b6be14808b32b300`

- **Disposition:** Corrected
- **Reason:** written from nes manual and checked claim by claim against it
- **Evidence:** NES manual "Mermaids of Atlantis - The Riddle of the Magic Bubble (USA) (Unl)" (archive.org NESManuals, OCR)
- **Change:** "so that four or matching colored spheres make contact" → "so that four or more matching colored spheres make contact" (Grammar: a dropped word; the manual says four or more of the same color.)
- **Check:** claim checker pass (10 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Twenty-four underwater stages structure the progression of the game, with settings selectable for easy, hard, or stacked configurations. Clusters of colorful spheres drift upward toward the top of an aquatic cavern. Players maneuver these rising formations so that four or matching colored spheres make contact. Upon touching, internal pressure builds up and causes the matched group to burst, freeing up space within the enclosure. If groups accumulate without clearing in time, the cavern completely fills and brings the session to an end. ¶ Irregular alignments leave empty voids known as air pockets, which consume space just like solid spheres. These voids can be eliminated either by sliding pieces into them or by removing spheres below to expose them directly to open water. Clusters occasionally arrive carrying a mystic pearl, which alters adjacent spheres into a single uniform color when positioned strategically.

**After (in production):**

### Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles II - The Arcade Game: `368d7ea3a066d4dc`

- **Disposition:** Corrected

- **Check:** claim checker pass (15 claims, 0 unsupported)

**Before:**

> Ten levels of action span across various locations throughout New York and cosmic alien hideouts, each concluding with an encounter against a lethal boss. Players select either Leonardo, Raphael, Michaelangelo, or Donatello, each outfitted with their signature weapons such as katana blades, sai daggers, nunchakus, or a bo staff. The mission involves fighting through hordes of Foot Soldiers and assorted enemy machines to rescue April O'Neil and confront Shredder in the Technodrome. ¶ Turtles can walk in eight directions, jump, attack with their weapons, or combine movements to deliver jumping kicks. Wounds deplete a turtle's life meter, though consuming pizzas restores energy. Each turtle begins with three lives, with bonus lives earned after defeating two hundred opponents. The game supports single-player action or two-player cooperative play. If a player exhausts their supply of lives during two-player mode, an extra life can be borrowed from their partner. Up to three continues are available to resume the adventure.

**After (in production):**

### Thunder & Lightning: `e3be0290f198fcee`

- **Disposition:** Corrected

**Before:**

> Thunder & Lightning is a block-breaking action game. Players control Mr. Chin, moving horizontally to deflect the Demon Ball with a stick and vaporize the layers forming the Wall of Regret. Striking flying saucers, airplanes, and submarines uncovers power-up items that drop into the playfield. Available enhancements include extending the paddle stick, catching and releasing the ball, launching missiles, slowing ball speed, enlarging the ball, granting extra lives, or dividing the sphere into multiple projectiles. ¶ Various creatures interfere with the ball and paddle during stages, such as an octopus that slows movement, turtles that generate fresh blocks, or the Thundersanrior firing lightning bolts. The title offers three play configurations: a single-player game, a two-player alternating mode with separate scores and screens, and a two-player simultaneous option where both participants share the screen and cooperate with combined scores.
